Title of the Invention

Geometric ExistenceBased Governance Architecture

Technical Field

The present invention relates to the technical field of computerimplemented systems for controlling the admissibility, execution, and protection of system effects in computing environments, and more particularly to architectures for governing when and how computational effects, data access, device functions, or service outputs may occur without reliance on identity recognition, credential verification, or policybased authorization decisions.

The invention lies within the technical domains of computerimplemented security and governance architectures, execution and interaction mediation systems, constraintbased and geometrybased computation frameworks for admissibility evaluation, and stateful, multiagent, distributed, and federated computing systems. In this context, the invention concerns technical substrates for governing execution, interaction, and system effects in software and hardware environments through structural admissibility conditions rather than through explicit decision logic.

The invention further relates, in a technical and comparative sense, to device security and execution control systems, authentication and authorization mechanisms, secure execution environments and isolation mechanisms, and servicelevel and outputlevel governance systems for software and networked services. Such related fields are referenced for technical context and contrast, and do not limit the scope of the invention.

More specifically, the invention relates to existencebased governance architectures in which the admissibility of system effects is determined by the satisfaction of invariant relational constraints and enforced through the binary existence or nonexistence of execution contexts, rather than through explicit authentication, authorization, access control lists, or policy evaluation mechanisms. Under such architectures, system effects are permitted to occur only when an admissible execution context exists and otherwise do not occur by virtue of nonexistence.

The technical field includes, but is not limited to, systems concerned with protection of data and computational state against unauthorized exposure, control of execution contexts in operating systems, devices, and distributed environments, governance of service and artificial intelligence outputs, enforcement of admissibility constraints in the presence of adversarial interaction or resource pressure, and prevention of domination or coercion by any single component, user, or external system.

The invention further relates to geometric and constraintbased evaluation techniques applied to input measurements or interaction contexts, wherein admissibility is assessed through invariant relational coherence rather than through comparison to stored representations, templates, exemplars, or reference identities. Such evaluation is nonrepresentational and does not involve ranking, similarity scoring, or optimization against stored reference data.

The invention is applicable to singledevice, multidevice, distributed, and federated computing systems, including softwareonly implementations, hardwareassisted implementations, and hybrid systems, and is independent of any particular application domain, data type, user role, deployment context, or industry.

The invention does not relate to, and does not claim, ethical judgments, legal rules or compliance frameworks, governance frameworks in the normative, organizational, or institutional sense, normative policy decisions, social rules, moral prescriptions, or valuebased determinations. Any philosophical, conceptual, or illustrative language used in this specification is nonoperative, nonnormative, and nonclaimed. The invention is limited exclusively to technical mechanisms implemented in computing systems.

The invention defines a general technical construction for governing admissibility, execution, and system effects in computing environments. Specific applications, including but not limited to data protection, device security, biometric interaction, artificial intelligence output governance, service interaction mediation, and policy enforcement contexts, are exemplary embodiments and do not limit the scope of the invention. The scope of protection is defined solely by the claims, as interpreted in light of this specification and the accompanying figures.

All terms used in this specification are employed consistently throughout the document. Where terms are expressly defined, such definitions override colloquial, dictionary, or ordinary meanings. Mathematical, geometric, and formal terms are used operationally to describe technical mechanisms and are not used metaphorically. Diagrams and figures are illustrative and nonlimiting. Terms such as “envelope,” “geometry,” “closure,” “admissibility,” “existence,” and “nonexistence” refer to formal technical constructs defined within this specification and are not intended to invoke abstract, philosophical, or nontechnical interpretations.

This application is filed in English. Any translations, if provided, are for convenience only and do not alter the substantive content of the disclosure.

Background / Prior Art

Modern computing systems are inherently stateful. Such systems maintain persistent state across time in the form of stored data, configuration information, firmware state, application state, longlived services, machinelearning models, and generated service outputs. This state persists across executions and sessions, and multiple actors—including users, processes, services, and external systems—may interact with the same underlying state. Interactions with stateful systems are mediated through technical interfaces such as application programming interfaces, device drivers, service endpoints, and user interfaces.

Security in stateful computing systems therefore concerns not only the correctness of credentials or the absence of software defects, but the technical structure by which state is accessed, transformed, combined, and shared once interactions are permitted. The manner in which authorized interactions proceed over time has a direct impact on how system effects arise and propagate across data, devices, and services.

Computerimplemented systems that control access to data, execution contexts, devices, or services have historically relied on identitycentric, policycentric, or detectioncentric security architectures. In such systems, the admissibility of system effects—such as disclosure of data, execution of code, activation of device functions, or generation of service outputs—is typically determined by explicit decision logic operating over representations of identity, credentials, permissions, rules, or behavioral classifications.

A first and widely deployed class of priorart systems determines admissibility through authentication of an asserted identity, followed by authorization based on that identity. Such systems include passwordbased authentication, tokenbased authentication, publickey authentication, and biometric authentication systems that compare captured inputs against stored reference templates. In these systems, an identity or credential is explicitly represented in system state, an incoming interaction is evaluated by comparing presented information against stored references, and admissibility is granted or denied through an authoritative decision point. This class of systems is well documented in international standards and practice, including identity and credential management frameworks such as ISO/IEC 27001, NIST SP 80063, and authentication and authorization protocols such as Kerberos and OAuth 2.0 (RFC 6749). Identitybased systems inherently expose interactive authentication surfaces, require credential lifecycle management, and rely on explicit allow/deny semantics.

A second class of priorart systems governs admissibility through policy evaluation, independent of how identity is established. Such systems include accesscontrol list mechanisms, rolebased access control, attributebased access control, and policy engines embedded in operating systems, databases, and enterprise services. In these systems, policies are explicitly encoded as rules, requests are evaluated against those rules, and admissibility is determined by a policy decision function that issues allow or deny outcomes. Policybased systems are described extensively in standards and architectures such as NIST RBAC models, XACML specifications, and operatingsystem accesscontrol frameworks. While such systems may be combined with cryptographic enforcement mechanisms, admissibility remains a function of decision logic, and policy engines constitute authoritative components capable of dominating or overriding other system behavior.

A third class of priorart systems addresses security by detecting, classifying, and mitigating undesirable behavior. These systems include intrusion detection systems, intrusion prevention systems, anomalydetection mechanisms, ratelimiting systems, and adaptive defense frameworks. In such systems, incoming interactions are monitored and classified as benign or malicious, detected events trigger mitigation actions such as blocking, throttling, escalation, or quarantine, and system behavior adapts over time based on observed patterns. Detectioncentric systems are widely documented in academic literature and industry practice and are commonly deployed alongside authentication and authorization mechanisms. By definition, such systems operate after an interaction has occurred and presuppose the existence of the interactions they observe.

Biometric systems in the prior art typically operate by extracting features from biometric inputs and comparing those features against stored templates using similarity metrics and thresholds, as described in standards such as ISO/IEC 19794 and related biometric data interchange formats. In these systems, biometric identity is represented explicitly as a stored template, admissibility depends on distance metrics and threshold comparisons, and partial similarity information may be inferred through repeated interaction. These characteristics introduce representational identity, ranking or scoring semantics, and observable gradients.

Across the foregoing categories, priorart systems share common structural characteristics. Admissibility is determined by explicit decisions rather than by the structure of interactions themselves; identity, credentials, policies, or classifications are represented in persistent system state; failure or restriction is typically signaled through denial messages, error codes, or mitigative actions; and components exercising decision authority mediate system behavior. Once an interaction is deemed authorized, subsequent behavior typically proceeds with broad discretion within the authorized context. The internal structure of authorized interactions is not continuously constrained, and asymmetries may accumulate as persistent state is accessed, transformed, or combined over time.

For example, an authorized database client may issue queries that aggregate or infer sensitive information; an authorized device process may modify configuration or firmware state in a persistent manner; or an authorized service may generate outputs that influence downstream systems or decisions beyond an intended scope. These behaviors arise from the structure of authorized interactions rather than from unauthorized access.

Detectionbased security mechanisms further exhibit a reactive character. A system that detects undesired interactions must, at least transiently, permit those interactions to exist in order to observe them. Detection therefore presupposes the existence of the events it seeks to classify, irrespective of the effectiveness of subsequent mitigation.

In addition, existing security solutions are typically fragmented across domains. Data security, device security, service governance, and control of artificialintelligence outputs are commonly treated as separate technical problems, addressed by different toolchains and enforcement models. Existing architectures do not generally provide a single, unified technical mechanism for constraining interactions across heterogeneous stateful systems.

The applicant is not aware of prior systems in which the admissibility of system effects is enforced solely through binary existence or nonexistence of execution contexts; invariant relational coherence is evaluated without representational identity, stored templates, or reference comparison; authority to grant or deny admissibility is structurally absent; and all inadmissibility, attack, distortion, or overload conditions resolve uniformly through nonexistence without detection, classification, or mitigation logic. Existing systems instead rely on combinations of identity recognition, policy evaluation, or adaptive response mechanisms, each of which introduces explicit decision points, authoritative control structures, persistent security state, or observable response behavior.

The classifications and structural characteristics described above can be independently validated by reference to widely adopted standards and specifications, including ISO/IEC 27001 (information security management systems), NIST SP 80063 (digital identity guidelines), RFC 6749 (OAuth 2.0 authorization framework), ISO/IEC 19794 (biometric data interchange formats), NIST RBAC models, and XACML policy specifications. These references demonstrate that priorart systems rely on identitybased, policybased, or detectionbased mechanisms rather than on existencebased enforcement of interaction structure.

In view of the foregoing, there exists an objective technical problem of providing a security architecture for stateful computing systems that constrains interactions with persistent state such that authorized interactions do not, by their structure, enable irreversible asymmetric influence, extraction, or dependency across data, devices, and services, while remaining applicable across heterogeneous computing environments.

Problem to Be Solved

Existing computerimplemented systems for controlling access to data, execution contexts, devices, or services suffer from structural limitations that arise from the manner in which admissibility of system effects is determined and enforced.

In particular, prior art systems determine admissibility through explicit decision mechanisms, such as identity verification, credential comparison, policy evaluation, or behavioral classification. These mechanisms introduce authoritative decision points into system architectures, which in turn give rise to technical problems that are independent of any particular application domain.

4.1 Structural Dependence on Representational Identity

A first technical problem arises from the reliance of prior art systems on representational identity, credentials, or templates stored in system state.

In such systems:

admissibility depends on the comparison of incoming inputs against stored representations;

identity or authorization information must persist across time;

and system behavior is conditioned on the correctness of those representations.

This structure necessarily introduces:

persistent sensitive state that must be protected;

attack surfaces associated with storage, comparison, and lifecycle management of identity data;

and failure modes in which partial similarity, ranking, or thresholding semantics may be inferred through interaction.

These characteristics are inherent to identitybased and templatebased systems as documented in authentication standards and biometric specifications, and they persist regardless of cryptographic strength.

4.2 Authoritative Decision Points and Domination Risk

A second technical problem arises from the presence of authoritative decision components that explicitly grant or deny admissibility.

In prior art systems:

authentication modules, policy engines, or access controllers issue allow/deny decisions;

those decisions dominate downstream system behavior;

and control over admissibility is concentrated in identifiable components.

This concentration of authority creates technical risks including:

single points of failure or compromise;

escalation paths through which components may override or bypass constraints;

and the possibility of coercive interaction, in which repeated or adaptive inputs influence decision outcomes.

These risks are structural properties of decisionbased architectures and are not eliminated by adding additional layers of policy, encryption, or monitoring.

4.3 Interactive Attack and Observation Surfaces

A further technical problem arises from the fact that prior art systems expose interactive surfaces through which admissibility is negotiated, tested, or observed.

Typical systems provide:

explicit denial messages, error codes, or status responses;

observable differences between failure modes;

retry semantics, thresholds, or escalation behavior.

As a result, system behavior may be probed, inferred, or optimized against through repeated interaction, even in the absence of successful compromise.

This problem is inherent to systems that distinguish between different categories of failure or that emit semantic feedback, as documented in security literature concerning oracle attacks, sidechannel leakage, and adaptive exploitation.

4.4 Detection and MitigationDriven Complexity

A further technical problem arises from the reliance of prior art systems on detection and mitigation mechanisms to handle attacks, misuse, or overload conditions.

Such systems:

classify inputs or behaviors as benign or malicious;

maintain adaptive state based on observed activity;

and apply mitigations such as blocking, throttling, or escalation.

This introduces:

additional processing complexity under attack conditions;

adaptive behavior that evolves over time;

and feedback loops that may themselves be exploited or become sources of error.

These characteristics are intrinsic to detectioncentric architectures and are documented in intrusion detection and prevention systems.

4.5 Lack of Uniform Failure Semantics

Across prior art approaches, failure is typically handled through nonuniform semantics, including:

explicit denial responses;

differentiated error states;

degraded modes of operation;

or partial access outcomes.

As a consequence, systems must manage and reason about multiple failure states, increasing complexity and the risk of unintended information disclosure.

4.6 Absence of ExistenceBased Governance in Prior Art

Taken together, the above characteristics reveal the absence in prior art of a system architecture in which:

admissibility of system effects is enforced through binary existence or nonexistence of execution contexts, rather than through decisions;

invariant relational coherence is evaluated without representational identity, comparison, or ranking;

no component possesses authority to grant, deny, or negotiate admissibility;

and all inadmissible interactions, distortions, attacks, or overload conditions resolve uniformly without detection, mitigation, or adaptive behavior.

Accordingly, there exists a technical need for a system architecture that:

governs admissibility, authority, and protection structurally rather than through decision logic;

eliminates persistent identity and policy state from admissibility determination;

removes interactive attack and observation surfaces;

and provides uniform, nonadaptive failure semantics.

4.7 Objective Technical Problem

The objective technical problem to be solved is therefore to provide a computerimplemented architecture that governs the admissibility of system effects in a manner that:

does not rely on identity recognition, credential verification, policy evaluation, or behavioral classification;

avoids authoritative decision points and coercive interaction;

prevents domination by any component through structural enforcement;

and resolves all inadmissibility, attack, or overload conditions through uniform, noninteractive behavior.

This problem is independent of application domain and arises in any system requiring controlled execution, access, or output under adversarial or uncertain conditions.

Summary of the Invention

The present invention provides a computerimplemented architectural system for governing the admissibility, execution, and protection of system effects in computing environments through existencebased enforcement, rather than through identity recognition, credential verification, policy evaluation, or behavioral classification.

In contrast to prior systems that determine admissibility by issuing explicit allow or deny decisions, the invention enforces admissibility by controlling whether an execution context exists or does not exist. System effects—including access to data, execution of code, activation of device functions, or generation of service outputs—are permitted to occur only through the existence of such execution contexts and are otherwise prevented by nonexistence. Within this specification, ‘existence’ denotes the condition under which a relational geometry is admissible within a constrained and temporally coherent state space. This usage is strictly computational and does not imply identity recognition or representational equivalence.

The full stack solution outlined in the commercial applications develops what is being coined as an “Admissibility System.” This lies above the operational level as an existence-based determinant for what computational operations may be executed by governing the existence of computational states rather than the execution of computational operations.

5.1 Core Architectural Composition

The invention is realized as a Geometric ExistenceBased Governance Architecture, comprising a coordinated set of technical elements that operate together as a single admissibility mechanism.

In particular, the architecture includes:

Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG), configured to deterministically evaluate whether input measurements or interaction contexts admit a coherent, invariant relational structure, without storing, comparing, ranking, or matching representational identities, templates, or reference data;

A Construction Environment defining invariant admissibility conditions under which execution contexts may exist, independent of user identity, credentials, or policy rules;

An envelopebased execution mechanism in which execution contexts are instantiated only when both geometric coherence and temporal admissibility are satisfied, and in which all inadmissible conditions resolve through collapse to nonexistence;

A temporal coherence mechanism, implemented using an existence history, that enforces nonforking admissibility over time without adaptive learning, tuning, or behavioral evolution;

Standingpreserving enforcement semantics, ensuring that no component, user, or external system possesses authority to grant, deny, override, or negotiate admissibility, thereby preventing domination or coercion within the system;

Projectiononly interaction surfaces, including user interfaces and external system interfaces, that do not participate in admissibility determination and expose no interrogative, authoritative, or decisionmaking behavior.

The invention lies in the enforced interaction of these elements as a unified architectural stack, rather than in any individual element taken in isolation.

5.2 ExistenceBased Enforcement and Collapse Semantics

Under the disclosed architecture, admissibility is binary and structural.

When invariant relational coherence and temporal admissibility are satisfied, an execution context exists and system effects may occur. When any invariant is violated—whether due to invalid input, distortion, adversarial interaction, resource exhaustion, or environmental failure—the execution context does not exist, and no system effect occurs.

All inadmissible conditions resolve uniformly through collapse to nonexistence, without generating denial messages, error codes, diagnostic output, or mitigation behavior. Collapse is noninteractive, nonadaptive, and computationally bounded.

5.3 NonDominance, NonCoercion, and Protection by NonExistence

The architecture enforces admissibility without relying on authoritative decisions or coercive interaction.

No component determines admissibility by evaluating claims, credentials, policies, or classifications. Instead, admissibility emerges solely from the satisfaction of invariant conditions. As a result:

no persistent identity or policy state is required for admissibility determination;

no component can dominate system behavior by issuing overrides or exceptions;

and data and system state are protected structurally by never becoming accessible when admissibility conditions are not met.

Protection is achieved not by encryption alone, but by preventing the existence of execution contexts through which protected effects could occur.

5.4 Attack, Distortion, and Availability Handling

The architecture treats distortion, adversarial behavior, misuse, and denialofservice conditions uniformly as inadmissible constructions.

Such conditions manifest as violations of invariant relational or temporal constraints and therefore resolve through nonexistence, without detection logic, mitigation logic, escalation paths, or adaptive response. No exploit state can persist because no partial or degraded execution context exists.

Availability pressure does not alter system behavior or introduce new semantics; when resources are available, admissible execution contexts may exist, and when resources are unavailable, execution contexts do not exist.

5.5 DomainIndependent Applicability

The disclosed architecture is independent of application domain and applies uniformly to:

data protection and controlled data access,

device and operatingsystem level execution control,

governance of service and artificialintelligence outputs,

policy enforcement substrates,

and distributed or federated computing environments.

The same existencebased enforcement semantics apply regardless of whether the system operates on a single device, across multiple devices, or in distributed systems.

5.6 Variants and Derivative Architectures

The invention explicitly encompasses derivative and functionally equivalent architectures in which the functional roles of geometric coherence evaluation, existencebased enforcement, standing preservation, temporal admissibility, and collapse semantics are distributed, reordered, decomposed, or substituted across components, provided that the same core invariants and technical effects are preserved.

Systems that introduce representational identity, comparison, ranking, authoritative decision points, or adaptive admissibility behavior are excluded from the scope of the invention, even if they superficially resemble an embodiment.

5.7 Technical Effects

By enforcing admissibility through existence-based governance rather than decision-based control, the invention achieves domain-independent technical effects including:

• elimination of interactive authentication and authorization surfaces;
• removal of identity representation from admissibility determination;
• binary, constant-cost failure semantics;
• prevention of domination and coercion by any system component;
• uniform handling of distortion, attack, and overload conditions;
• reduction of operational and cognitive complexity;
• reduction in computational overhead associated with repeated validation;
• improved resource efficiency in computational systems;
• and enhanced energy efficiency with corresponding environmental and economic benefits.

These effects arise directly from the disclosed architecture and do not depend on application-specific logic.

In conventional identity and authentication systems, admissibility is determined through repeated evaluation of credentials, tokens, or biometric representations. This requires continuous execution of cryptographic validation, protocol negotiation, and policy evaluation for each interaction, session, or access request. As a result, computational cost scales with the number of interactions between system participants.

In contrast, the present invention determines admissibility as a function of relational closure over system configuration. Validation is therefore performed only upon changes to system structure, including device admission, removal, or modification, rather than per interaction. This results in a fundamental shift in computational scaling behavior, such that processing cost is proportional to the rate of topology change rather than interaction frequency.

In large-scale deployments, this distinction leads to a reduction in validation operations by multiple orders of magnitude. Systems comprising tens of thousands to millions of devices may require hundreds of millions to billions of authentication operations annually under conventional architectures. Under the present invention, equivalent systems require recomputation only upon structural changes, which occur significantly less frequently.

This reduction in computational workload produces a direct reduction in energy consumption. Because each validation operation incurs processor utilization, memory access, and network overhead, elimination of repeated validation results in measurable decreases in total system energy demand. In representative enterprise-scale systems, this corresponds to reductions on the order of megawatt-hours annually, while in large-scale distributed systems the reductions scale proportionally.

In data center environments, reductions in computational workload further produce secondary reductions in energy consumption associated with cooling, power distribution, and infrastructure overhead. Given typical power usage effectiveness (PUE) characteristics, decreases in compute demand propagate to total facility energy savings, improving overall system efficiency.

From a system-wide perspective, security-related control-plane operations (including authentication, authorization, and policy evaluation) constitute a measurable fraction of total computational workload. By eliminating repeated interaction-driven validation, the present invention reduces this control-plane overhead, resulting in total data center energy savings on the order of a fractional percentage to several percent, depending on system configuration and workload composition.

These reductions correspond to measurable decreases in carbon emissions in large-scale deployments and contribute to improved environmental sustainability. At the same time, reduced computational demand lowers infrastructure utilization, resulting in decreased hardware load, reduced cooling requirements, and extended system lifespan.

Importantly, these improvements are achieved not through optimization of individual operations, but through architectural elimination of entire classes of repeated computation. The invention replaces continuous validation processes with discrete structural admissibility conditions, thereby reducing both computational intensity and system complexity.

In addition to environmental benefits, the reduction in computational workload yields direct economic advantages. Lower processor utilization, reduced energy consumption, and decreased cooling demand translate into reduced operational costs in both enterprise and large-scale data center environments. These cost reductions scale with system size and interaction frequency, providing increasing economic benefit in high-volume deployments.

Accordingly, the invention provides a unified improvement in system security, computational efficiency, resource utilization, and environmental impact, arising directly from the use of closure-based relational governance.

Brief Description of the Figures

FIG. A shows an admissible existence region within a construction environment representing an execution context space, wherein a set of invariant geometric constraints defines an envelope as the intersection of those constraints, such that execution contexts within the envelope exist and execution contexts outside the envelope do not exist and therefore cannot produce system effects, independent of identity, policy, or authoritative decision.

FIG. B shows temporal coherence of existence without dynamics in a construction environment, wherein invariant admissibility rules define a fixed admissible existence region over time, execution contexts exist only at discrete time instants corresponding to membership in the admissible region, no execution context exists outside those instants, no state is retained between existence events, and no learning, adaptation, rule updates, or forked histories are permitted.

FIG. C shows structural nondominance and absence of admissibility authority within a construction environment, wherein an admissible existence region is defined by invariant structure, execution contexts exist only within that region, system effects occur only as a consequence of such existence, and users, policies, administrators, user interfaces, and external systems are structurally excluded from granting, denying, or overriding admissibility.

FIG. D shows structural stack components realizing geometric existencebased governance, wherein a construction environment defines invariant admissibility rules, interoperability constraint geometry enforces invariant relational constraints, an admissible existence region is defined as an envelope, existence semantics provide that execution contexts exist only by membership in the admissible region, temporal coherence preserves the admissible region over time without learning or adaptation, system effects occur only when an execution context exists, and no decision function or actor is capable of granting, denying, or overriding admissibility.

FIG. E shows a unified nonexistence outcome for inadmissible conditions together with zerovisibility and oraclefree interaction semantics within a construction environment having invariant admissibility rules, wherein multiple attempted contexts—including invalid geometry, replay attempts, maninthemiddle distortion, resource exhaustion, and unauthorized contexts—lie outside an admissible existence region and therefore do not give rise to any execution context, system effects occur only from execution contexts that exist within the admissible existence region, and external actors are unable to observe, infer, or differentiate inadmissible conditions due to the absence of denial, detection, mitigation, escalation, feedback, sidechannels, error signals, timing signals, or other observable responses.

FIG. G shows nonrepresentational geometry evaluation within a construction environment having invariant admissibility rules, wherein an attempted context is represented solely by intrinsic relational structure comprising multiple internal relations, invariant constraints evaluate only internal relational coherence of the attempted context without comparison to any stored reference data, templates, identities, or exemplars, admissibility is determined as a binary outcome without ranking, thresholds, optimization, distance metrics, or similarity scoring, and reference data is explicitly excluded from participation in admissibility determination.

FIG. H shows equivalence of derivative implementations preserving invariant existencebased governance semantics, wherein a monolithic implementation, a distributed or decomposed implementation, and a reordered or embedded implementation differ in component arrangement, naming, and distribution while preserving admissibility, existence semantics, nondominance, nonrepresentational geometry evaluation, and temporal invariance, such that all implementations are semantically equivalent when the same invariants are preserved.

FIG. I shows domainindependent application of invariant existencebased governance semantics, wherein the same admissibility, existence semantics, nondominance, nonrepresentational geometry evaluation, and temporal invariance apply unchanged across different embodiment classes including data effects, device effects, AI output effects, and policy effects, such that only the effect target differs while the governing semantics remain identical.

FIG. J shows external interoperability with nontransitive trust within an existencebased governance system, wherein a trusted device environment contains an admissible existence region enclosing sensitive data, external services are separated by a nontransitive trust boundary and receive only outputlevel exposure from the admissible existence region, no direct data access or data visibility is provided to external services, and attempted userdriven coercive disclosure constitutes a geometrically inadmissible event that causes the admissible existence region to cease to exist without policy decisions, warnings, mitigation, or escalation. Protected data exists only inside the envelope. Envelope outputs exist inside the device and are visible to the user.
External services see nothing unless an admissible output is explicitly introduced.
Until then, there is nothing for them to recognize or take.

FIG. K shows composition of existencebased governance with foundational security layers within a computing system, wherein supplychain security, secure or measured boot, hardware isolation and sidechannel resistance, and cryptographic primitives provide integrity, confidentiality, and physical correctness, and an existencebased governance layer composes above such foundational layers to define admissibility and action existence without replacing or enforcing cryptographic, hardware, or bootlevel guarantees, such that lower security layers ensure integrity of execution while the existencebased governance layer determines which actions and data couplings may exist.

Detailed Description of the Invention

7.1 Definitions and Terminology

The following definitions are provided to establish consistent and precise usage of terms throughout this specification. Unless otherwise indicated, the definitions set forth herein govern the interpretation of the claims, description, and figures. Where a defined term is used, the definition provided herein supersedes any colloquial, dictionary, or customary meaning of the term.

As used herein, the term “system effect” refers to any observable or consequential outcome produced by a computing system, including but not limited to access to data, execution of code, modification of system state, activation of device functions, generation of service outputs, or emission of externally visible signals.

The term “execution context” refers to a technical state in which one or more system effects are capable of occurring. An execution context is not a process, thread, session, or authorization grant, but a condition of realizability under which system effects may be instantiated.

The term “existence”, when applied to an execution context, denotes that the technical conditions required for the realization of one or more system effects are satisfied. Conversely, the term “nonexistence” denotes that such conditions are not satisfied and that the corresponding execution context is not realizable. Nonexistence is not a denial, failure, error, or exception, but the absence of a realizable execution context.

The term “admissibility” refers to the property that determines whether an execution context may exist. Admissibility is evaluated structurally and is binary in nature, such that an execution context either exists or does not exist. Admissibility is not determined by explicit decisions, authorizations, permissions, or policy evaluations.

The term “construction environment” refers to the technical environment within which admissibility is evaluated and execution contexts may exist. The construction environment defines invariant conditions under which execution contexts are realizable and is independent of user identity, credentials, or policy rules.

The term “envelope” refers to an admissible execution context within the construction environment. An envelope does not contain data as a container, does not represent a security boundary enforced by policy, and does not persist as a session or state. The envelope exists only while admissibility conditions are satisfied and ceases to exist when such conditions are not satisfied.

The term “closure”, when used in connection with an envelope or execution context, refers to the transition from existence to nonexistence of that execution context. Closure is structural and noninteractive and does not produce denial messages, error codes, alerts, or mitigation actions.

The term “geometric”, as used herein, refers to the evaluation of invariant relational structure among elements of an interaction context. Geometric evaluation does not involve spatial geometry, metaphors, or visualization, and does not rely on representational identities, stored templates, exemplars, similarity metrics, distance measures, ranking, or optimization.

The term “relational coherence” refers to the satisfaction of invariant relationships among elements of an interaction or execution context. Relational coherence is evaluated without reference to prior instances, historical state, or learned models.

The term “nonrepresentational” refers to the absence of stored identities, templates, exemplars, profiles, or reference data used to determine admissibility. Evaluation is performed solely on the current interaction context and its relational structure.

The term “standing” refers to the capacity of any component, user, or external system to authoritatively grant, deny, override, or negotiate admissibility. The term “nondominant” refers to enforcement semantics in which no such standing exists and admissibility is not subject to authoritative control.

The term “projection surface” refers to an interface, including a user interface or external system interface, that renders or transmits outputs of an admissible execution context without participating in admissibility determination or enforcement.

The term “external system” refers to any system, service, or component outside the construction environment, including but not limited to networked services, softwareasaservice platforms, artificial intelligence services, or collaboration systems.

The term “nontransitive trust” refers to the property that trust or admissibility within the construction environment does not propagate to external systems. External systems do not gain access to internal data or state by virtue of receiving outputs.

The term “coercive interaction” refers to an attempted interaction whose structure would, if realized, cause protected system effects to be externalized or coupled in a manner that violates admissibility conditions. A coercive interaction does not result in partial execution or failure, but in nonexistence of the corresponding execution context.

The term “temporal coherence” refers to the property that admissibility conditions are invariant across time and do not adapt, learn, or evolve based on past interactions. No historical security state, behavioral profile, or failure memory is retained for the purpose of admissibility determination.

The term “oracle behavior” refers to any observable signal, response, or variation that allows inference of admissibility boundaries through interaction. The disclosed system exposes no oracle behavior.

Unless otherwise stated, references to “data,” “devices,” “services,” or “outputs” are illustrative of classes of system effects and do not limit the scope of the invention.

7.2 General Architecture (Domain Independent)

The general architecture described in this section constitutes a domainindependent structural instantiation of the semantic constructs defined in Section 3. Every architectural element described herein is an instantiation, composition, or organizational grouping of the primitives System, State, Actor, Interaction, Operation, Constraint, Envelope, Admissibility, and Closure, as previously defined. No architectural element introduced in this section is to be interpreted as a new semantic primitive, and no semantic meaning is created, modified, or extended by the architectural description.

Architecture, as described herein, specifies how the semantic constructs defined in Section 3 are realized and coordinated within a computing system. Semantics remain exclusively defined in Section 3. The architecture does not redefine admissibility, existence, or closure, but provides a structural realization through which those semantics are enforced. The architectural construction applies identically regardless of the type of State, the type of Actor, or the application domain, and is therefore domainindependent.

7.2.1 System Boundary and Control Scope

The System is architecturally delimited by a boundary of control that encloses all mechanisms responsible for representing State, receiving Interaction proposals, evaluating Constraints, instantiating Envelopes, and executing Operations. This boundary defines the maximal extent of architectural control and identifies the set of interfaces, execution paths, and state representations governed by the System.

All architectural interfaces through which Actors interact with the System terminate at this boundary. Controlled execution paths originate within the boundary and are reachable only through admissible Operations. Controlled state representations exist exclusively within the boundary and are not directly accessible from outside the System. This boundary constitutes a direct instantiation of the boundary of control defined in Section 3.

All admissibility evaluation occurs strictly within the System boundary. No Interaction originating outside the boundary is representable within the System, and no external process, service, or component can introduce or execute an Operation without first passing through the architectural mechanisms enclosed by the boundary.

7.2.2 Architectural Representation of State

State is represented architecturally as internal system structures maintained within the System boundary. State is not exposed directly to Actors and is not readable, writable, or duplicable except through mediated Operations executed within an Envelope. No architectural component provides raw access to State.

State representation is segmented by scope. Systemglobal State comprises invariant structures required for maintaining admissibility semantics and is not directly modifiable by Operations. Envelopescoped State comprises the subset of State accessible within a particular Envelope and is isolated from both Systemglobal State and the Envelopescoped State of other Envelopes. Envelopescoped State exists only for the lifetime of the corresponding Envelope and cannot be shared, merged, or reused across Envelopes.

7.2.3 Actor Interfaces and Interaction Entry Points

Actors initiate Interactions exclusively through defined architectural interfaces. An interface enables an Actor to propose an Interaction by submitting an Operation candidate. An interface does not grant permission, authority, or admissibility, and does not itself execute Operations or modify State.

The interaction proposal path consists of receiving an Interaction proposal, constructing an Operation candidate that specifies an intended transformation or access of State, and submitting that candidate for admissibility evaluation. At no point along this path does execution occur, and no State transition is realized prior to admissibility determination.

7.2.4 Envelope Instantiation Architecture

An Envelope is instantiated architecturally in response to an Interaction proposal and prior to the execution of any Operation. No Operation executes outside the context of an Envelope.

Each Envelope is composed of architectural structures representing:
(i) a Participant Set corresponding to the Actors involved in the Interaction;
(ii) a State Scope defining the Envelopescoped State accessible within the Envelope;
(iii) a Constraint Set corresponding to the Constraints applicable to the Interaction; and
(iv) an Admissible Operation Space defining the Operations that may be realized within the Envelope.
Each of these elements is a direct architectural instantiation of its corresponding semantic definition in Section 3.

Envelope contexts are isolated from one another. Envelopes do not share mutable State, do not exchange information, and do not communicate through side effects, shared memory, or implicit channels. The isolation of Envelopes ensures that admissibility and execution within one Envelope cannot influence or leak into another.

7.2.5 Constraint Evaluation Architecture

The System includes architectural components responsible for evaluating Constraints associated with an Interaction. Constraint evaluation is deterministic, decidable, and mechanical. Evaluation depends solely on the current Interaction context and its relational structure and does not rely on stored identities, historical interaction traces, adaptive models, or probabilistic inference.

Constraint evaluation is performed prior to execution of an Operation and, where applicable, continuously throughout the lifetime of an Envelope. Continuous evaluation ensures that admissibility conditions remain satisfied for the duration of execution without introducing adaptive behavior or temporal evolution of admissibility criteria.

Based on constraint evaluation, an Operation candidate is classified as admissible or nonadmissible. This classification is binary and admits no intermediate states.

7.2.6 Operation Execution Architecture

The architecture enforces an execution gate that permits only admissible Operations to reach execution. Nonadmissible Operations do not execute and do not produce partial effects, side effects, or residual state changes.

Execution occurs exclusively within the context of an Envelope. Operations executed within an Envelope are constrained to the Envelopescoped State and do not affect Systemglobal State except as explicitly permitted by admissible State transitions. Execution does not introduce implicit execution paths or side effects beyond the Envelope context.

7.2.7 Envelope State Space Management and Closure

Envelope State Space is tracked architecturally as the set of admissible State transitions reachable within the Envelope. Admissible Operations map transitions within this space. Transitions not represented within the Envelope State Space are unrepresentable and cannot occur.

Closure is enforced structurally. When no admissible State transitions remain, the Envelope State Space is empty and the Envelope collapses from existence to nonexistence. Closure is noninteractive and does not generate denial messages, error codes, alerts, or mitigative behavior.

7.2.8 Envelope Collapse Handling

Envelope collapse is detected architecturally as the absence of any admissible continuation within the Envelope State Space. Upon collapse, execution terminates immediately. No degraded execution occurs, no partial State changes persist, and no residual execution context remains. Recovery requires instantiation of a new Envelope through a new Interaction proposal.

7.2.9 Structural Distortion Handling

Structural distortion manifests architecturally as a violation of one or more Constraints. Distortion is not classified as an attack and is not detected through behavioral analysis or pattern recognition.

Distorted Interactions have no admissible continuation within the Envelope State Space and therefore cannot execute. Nonexecution follows directly from nonadmissibility.

7.2.10 NonExtractability Enforcement Architecture

Outputs produced by Operations remain bound to the Envelope in which they are produced. Outputs cannot be reused, duplicated, or externalized independently of the Envelope context.

The architecture structurally excludes raw read, raw export, and uncontrolled duplication of State or outputs. No architectural component provides a mechanism for extracting Envelopescoped State outside the Envelope context.

7.2.11 Authorization and Authentication Placement

Authentication and authorization, where present, occur architecturally as parameterization of Envelope instantiation and never as a bypass of admissibility Constraints. Authorization does not disable, override, or weaken constraint evaluation or admissibility determination.

7.2.12 ArchitectureLevel Failure Semantics

Architecturelevel failure classes include nonadmissible Operations, structural distortion, and Envelope collapse. All failure classes result uniformly in nonexecution, absence of side effects, and absence of partial State change.

7.2.13 Architecture Completeness and Consistency

The architecture constitutes a faithful and complete instantiation of the semantics defined in Section 3. No architectural element introduces hidden State access, side channels, implicit execution paths, or covert signaling mechanisms.

7.2.14 Rademacher Identity Closure Architecture (RICA)

The Rademacher Identity Closure Architecture (RICA) is a non-limiting architectural instantiation of the closure-based governance framework described herein. RICA provides a concrete mechanism by which the primitives of Envelopes, Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG), and Standing Algebra (ΣR) are applied to identity formation, system participation, and execution control within a distributed computing environment.

In the general framework of this invention, existence, admissibility, and execution are determined through closure over invariant relational constraints. RICA specializes this principle by constructing device identity directly from the relational configuration between a device and all other admitted participants, and by binding that configuration to both a closure envelope and a synchronized system-wide representation.

For a given device Dᵢ, a relational configuration Rᵢ is constructed as an ordered and typed set of interaction relations:

Rᵢ = {Rᵢ₁, Rᵢ₂, …, Rᵢₙ}

where each relation Rᵢⱼ encodes structured interaction properties including temporal ordering, state access dependencies, information flow symmetry, compositional constraints, and ΣR standing relationships. These relations correspond directly to axes defined under ICG and are therefore suitable for geometric admissibility evaluation.

The mechanism proceeds deterministically as follows:

(1) Relational Construction: Upon admission or system update, the relational configuration Rᵢ is constructed for each device using the current set of admitted devices and permitted interaction structures. This construction may be performed by authorized entities (e.g., registry devices) operating under ΣR constraints.

(2) Constraint Projection (ICG Mapping): The relational configuration Rᵢ is mapped into an ICG constraint set Cᵢ, wherein each relation contributes invariant conditions across interaction axes. This produces a closed admissible region representing all structurally valid configurations for Dᵢ.

(3) Closure Envelope Formation: From Cᵢ, an Envelope Eᵢ is computed as the closure of all admissible state transitions and system effects derivable from Rᵢ. The Envelope Eᵢ defines: (a) the admissible operation space Aᵢ, (b) the admissible state space Sᵢ, (c) the invariant future state space under all permissible transitions. Existence of Dᵢ is equivalent to the non-emptiness of Eᵢ.

(4) Relational Encoding (Ledger Projection): The relational configuration Rᵢ is encoded into a representation Hᵢ, such as a cryptographic hash:

Hᵢ = Hash(Rᵢ₁, Rᵢ₂, …, Rᵢₙ)

This representation is stored in a distributed ledger replicated across devices. The ledger does not store identity artifacts, but rather a synchronized projection of relational structure sufficient to verify consistency of Rᵢ across the system.

(5) Admission and Continuity Condition: A device Dᵢ is considered present in the system if and only if: (a) its locally derived relational configuration Rᵢ is consistent with the current system configuration, (b) its corresponding representation Hᵢ matches the ledger state, and (c) the closure envelope Eᵢ remains non-empty under ICG and ΣR constraints.

(6) Execution Enforcement: All operations involving Dᵢ must map to admissible transitions within Eᵢ. Any operation that violates the constraint set Cᵢ or ΣR conditions fails to produce a successor state and is therefore non-admissible. No partial execution or degraded state is permitted.

(7) Relational Mutation and Global Reconfiguration: When a device is admitted or removed, the global relational configuration is recomputed. For removal of a device Dₖ: (a) all relations Rᵢₖ are eliminated, (b) all relational configurations Rᵢ are reconstructed, (c) all constraint sets Cᵢ and envelopes Eᵢ are recomputed, (d) all representations Hᵢ are regenerated, (e) the updated ledger is distributed to all devices.

This induces a system-wide transformation of identity and admissible state spaces. Devices for which no valid closure envelope exists under the updated configuration undergo collapse to non-existence.

Within this architecture, identity is not a static or transferable attribute. It is the emergent result of a closure-complete relational configuration evaluated under invariant constraints. The ledger representation Hᵢ serves only as a synchronization and consistency mechanism; it is neither sufficient nor meaningful in isolation from the underlying relational structure.

RICA demonstrates that identity, authorization, and execution control may be implemented as a single closure-governed system in which: (i) identity corresponds to relational closure, (ii) admissibility corresponds to geometric constraint satisfaction, and (iii) execution corresponds to closure-preserving state transition.

It should be emphasized that the use of hashing, distributed ledgers, or specific device classes in this section represents one possible implementation of the broader invention. The inventive concept resides in the determination of existence and system effects through closure over relational configurations, independent of any particular encoding, storage, or synchronization mechanism.

Accordingly, alternative embodiments may derive identity directly from relational closure without use of cryptographic hashing or distributed ledgers, and such variations remain within the scope of the present invention.

7.3 Envelope Model (Core of the Invention)

The Envelope constitutes the core technical construct of the invention and defines the exclusive structural mechanism through which admissibility, execution, and termination of system effects are realized. The Envelope is defined semantically in Section 3, instantiated architecturally in Section 4, and is operationally specified in this section. All properties, behaviors, and transitions described herein are fully determined by the semantic constructs previously defined and do not introduce any additional primitives or interpretive constructs.

No securityrelevant Interaction, Operation, or State transformation exists outside the Envelope. Any Interaction not representable within an Envelope is undefined within the System and cannot occur.

7.3.1 Exclusivity and Structural Position

The Envelope is the exclusive unit of security enforcement within the System. All admissibility, execution, State transformation, and output generation occur strictly within the scope of an Envelope. No Interaction spans multiple Envelopes, and no Operation is executed across Envelope boundaries.

The System enforces admissibility at the level of interaction spaces rather than at the level of Actors, objects, or channels. As a result, admissibility is a property of relational structure and not of identity, role, or classification.

Any Interaction not representable as an Envelope is structurally undefined and therefore cannot be realized.

7.3.2 Authority and Construction

Envelopes are constructed exclusively by the System. Actors possess no authority to construct, modify, negotiate, or override Envelope composition.

Envelope construction is derived entirely from the structure of the proposed Interaction and from invariant System rules. No discretionary, heuristic, or policybased authority participates in Envelope formation.

The Envelope lifecycle consists of instantiation, evaluation, execution, evolution, and collapse or termination, each phase being deterministically governed by Constraint enforcement.

7.3.3 Instantiation and Deterministic Resolution

An Envelope is instantiated upon the proposal of an Interaction that would affect State, and always prior to execution of any Operation.

The Participant Set is resolved deterministically from the Interaction structure and System rules. No selection mechanism introduces asymmetry, preference, or discretionary inclusion.

The State Scope is determined solely by rulederived constraints. State elements not included in the Scope are not hidden or masked but are nonexistent within the Envelope context.

The Constraint Set is assembled at instantiation and is complete. It includes all structural, temporal, symmetry, and nondomination constraints necessary to preserve invariant system properties. No constraint is added or modified after instantiation.

The initial Envelope State Space is defined as the complete set of States reachable through admissible Operations under the Constraint Set.

7.3.4 Admissible Operation Space

The Admissible Operation Space is fully defined at instantiation. Continuous evaluation may reduce this space but does not expand it.

Admissibility is determined exclusively by structural and relational invariants. No representational identity, stored templates, similarity metrics, probabilistic scoring, or behavioral profiling contributes to admissibility determination.

The Admissible Operation Space is closed. Every admissible Operation maps to a subsequent State within the Envelope State Space. Any Operation that would violate closure is unrepresentable.

Admissibility is strictly binary. No partial admissibility, ranking, or gradient of acceptance exists.

7.3.5 Execution Semantics

No Operation executes outside an Envelope, prior to admissibility determination, or across Envelope boundaries.

Admissibility is evaluated both prior to execution and continuously during execution where required to preserve invariant structure. Such evaluation is nonadaptive and does not depend on prior interactions or accumulated history.

Operations are atomic with respect to admissibility. No partial execution or partial State transition is possible.

Execution is confined to Envelopescoped State and does not produce effects beyond the Envelope unless explicitly permitted by admissible transitions.

7.3.6 Envelope Evolution and Invariant Preservation

Execution of admissible Operations produces transitions within the Envelope State Space.

Envelope evolution is constrained by invariants ensuring:

preservation of admissible future State space (exit viability),

absence of irreversible asymmetric advantage,

bounded transformation of State,

symmetry preservation where applicable,

consistency of successor transitions.

Any Operation that would violate these invariants is nonadmissible and cannot execute.

State evolution is thus constrained such that no sequence of admissible Operations can produce structural asymmetry, irreversible dependency, or loss of admissible continuation.

7.3.7 Closure and Collapse

Closure is enforced structurally through the boundedness of the Envelope State Space. When no admissible Operations remain, the Envelope collapses from existence to nonexistence.

Collapse is immediate and noninteractive. No degraded execution, fallback, or partial continuation occurs. No intermediate or partial State persists.

Collapse is an enforcement outcome and reflects the exhaustion of admissible transitions rather than an error condition.

7.3.8 Structural Distortion Handling

Structural distortion manifests as violation of relational invariants. Distortion is not classified, detected, or interpreted as an external condition, but is represented solely as nonadmissibility.

Distorted Interactions have no admissible continuation and therefore cannot execute. No intermediate representation of distortion is exposed.

7.3.9 NonExtractability and Output Binding

All outputs remain bound to the Envelope in which they are produced. Outputs cannot be extracted, reused, or duplicated independently of the Envelope context.

No mechanism exists for raw read, raw export, or uncontrolled duplication of State. All observable outputs are mediated through Envelopebound projections.

7.3.10 Authorization and Authentication Placement

Authentication and authorization, where present, act only as parameters influencing Envelope instantiation. They do not grant admissibility independently and cannot override Constraints.

Admissibility remains structurally determined and invariant under all parameterizations.

7.3.11 Failure Semantics

All nonadmissible conditions, structural distortions, and Envelope collapses result uniformly in nonexecution, absence of side effects, and absence of partial State change.

No additional failure modes, recovery mechanisms, or mitigation behaviors are introduced.

7.3.12 Structural Invariants

The Envelope model enforces the following invariants throughout its operation:

admissibility is determined solely by structural relations within the current Interaction context;

no Operation reduces the admissible future State space below a nonempty threshold unless collapse occurs;

no admissible sequence of Operations produces irreversible asymmetric influence;

admissibility is invariant across time and does not evolve based on past interactions;

no representational identity or similarity metric participates in admissibility;

all admissible transitions preserve closure within the Envelope State Space;

no authority exists that can override admissibility determination.

7.3.13 Completeness and Consistency

The Envelope model constitutes a complete and faithful instantiation of the semantics defined in Section 3. All architectural elements correspond directly to those semantics and introduce no hidden State access, side channels, or implicit execution paths.

Admissibility precedes execution in all cases. Closure semantics are explicit and structurally enforced. Nonextractability is guaranteed by construction.

7.3.14 Novel Construction

Security is enforced by defining admissible interaction spaces, enforcing closure of admissible operations within those spaces, and treating nonadmissible interactions as undefined and therefore nonexecutable.

Prior systems permit interactions and subsequently react to violations. The present invention does not represent such violations within the System and therefore does not execute or mitigate them. This distinction is structural and arises from the definition of admissibility and existence.

Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG)

7.4.1 Binding Status

Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG) is defined in this section as a formally evaluable constraint framework and is specified as a technical mechanism used by the System to evaluate, prior to execution, the relational structure, composability, and admissibility of Interactions.

The mathematical formulation of ICG as an abstract geometric or algebraic system is not claimed in isolation. The present invention claims the technical application of ICG, implemented by an enforcement mechanism, to construct admissible interaction spaces, constrain interaction composition prior to execution, and enforce closure and collapse semantics within the Envelope model defined in Sections 3–5.

All constructs described herein rely exclusively on the semantic primitives defined in Section 3 and the architectural instantiation described in Section 4. No new semantic primitives are introduced.

7.4.2 Role of ICG Within the System

ICG provides the formal basis for evaluating whether an Interaction is structurally admissible. It operates by determining whether the relational configuration of an Interaction lies within a closed admissible region defined by invariant constraints.

The enforcement mechanism applies ICG to:

construct Envelope Constraint Sets,

define the Admissible Operation Space,

ensure that all admissible Operations preserve closure,

and determine the condition under which an Envelope collapses.

Without ICG, admissibility cannot be computed, as there would be no formal method to determine whether an Interaction satisfies the invariant structural conditions required for execution.

The term “geometric” denotes that admissibility is determined by relational structure and invariant relationships among elements of an Interaction. It does not imply spatial embedding, visualization, or reliance on Euclidean, metric, or vector space properties. The geometry may be discrete, symbolic, continuous, or mixed, provided that evaluation is mechanically decidable.

7.4.3 Attribution and Scope Clarification

The formal structure referred to herein as Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG) originates from prior work authored by the inventor. That prior work describes the abstract formulation of relational constraint systems and their invariants.

The present invention does not claim the abstract mathematical formulation of ICG. Instead, it claims the application of ICG as a technical mechanism within a computing system to enforce admissibility, constrain interaction composition, and ensure closure of system effects.

The inventor is the author of both the underlying formal framework and the present technical application. The present disclosure is selfcontained and does not rely on external documents for enablement.

7.4.4 Interaction Geometry

Each Interaction is represented as an element within a relational structure defined over multiple invariant axes. These axes do not correspond to numerical coordinates but to structural relations that must satisfy invariant conditions.

The relational axes include, without limitation:

participant relation structure,

State access relations,

Operation composition relations,

temporal ordering relations,

information flow symmetry relations.

Each axis represents a constraint domain in which admissibility is evaluated. These domains are not independent; admissibility depends on the joint satisfaction of invariants across all axes.

Interaction geometry is not reducible to graphs, vector spaces, policy lattices, or constraint satisfaction systems taken in isolation. Admissibility depends on composite invariants that are defined only within the closed context of an Envelope and cannot be evaluated independently of that context.

7.4.5 Interoperability Constraints

An interoperability constraint defines the conditions under which multiple Interaction geometries may intersect, compose, and evolve.

Two Interaction geometries intersect if and only if they are potentially interoperable. Intersection is a necessary condition but not sufficient for admissibility.

Admissibility requires:

satisfaction of all Envelope constraints,

preservation of invariant relational structure,

and satisfaction of symmetry and nondomination conditions.

Symmetry constraints ensure that no participant occupies a structurally privileged position unless such asymmetry is itself admissible under invariant conditions. This enforces structural nondomination through constraint satisfaction rather than policy.

7.4.6 Envelope Construction via ICG

The Envelope is constructed as a closed region within the interaction geometry.

The mapping is as follows:

Participant Set corresponds to a relational subspace of participant interactions;

State Scope corresponds to a region of accessible State within that space;

Admissible Operation Space corresponds to allowable transformations within the region;

Constraint Set corresponds to operators that restrict the region.

An Envelope is closed if all admissible transformations map to States within the same region.

Nonadmissible interactions correspond to elements outside this region. Such elements are not blocked or rejected; they are not representable within the System and therefore cannot be executed.

7.4.7 Collapse as Geometric Degeneracy

Envelope collapse occurs when the admissible region becomes empty.

This condition corresponds to the absence of any admissible transformations that preserve invariant structure. Collapse is therefore the structural consequence of constraint satisfaction, not a response to detected behavior.

Attempts to force incompatible Interaction geometries result in the elimination of admissible space and therefore collapse.

7.4.8 Continuous Enforcement Without Monitoring

ICG is evaluated prior to execution and, where applicable, continuously during execution. This evaluation constrains admissible evolution without observing or reacting to violations.

The enforcement mechanism does not:

monitor behavior after violation,

log or classify violations,

or apply reactive mitigation.

Instead, admissibility is determined structurally such that inadmissible Interactions cannot be realized.

Reduction of admissible space occurs as a direct consequence of invariant preservation, not as a result of monitoring or behavioral inference.

7.4.9 Relationship to Structural Invariants

ICG enforces structural invariants including:

preservation of admissible future states,

symmetry of interaction structure,

bounded transformation of State,

absence of irreversible asymmetric advantage,

invariance of admissibility across time.

These invariants are enforced through constraint evaluation and are independent of identity, policy, or behavioral classification.

An Interaction is admissible if and only if it lies within the admissible geometric region and preserves all invariant constraints.

7.4.10 DomainIndependent Applicability

ICG applies uniformly across all domains in which Interactions may be represented, including:

data systems,

device control systems,

service execution environments,

artificial intelligence interaction flows,

and authentication subsystems.

The framework constrains interaction admissibility rather than model behavior, training processes, or optimization procedures.

7.4.11 Novel Construction

The novelty lies in the use of a formal geometric constraint system to define admissible interaction spaces, enforce closure, and treat nonadmissible interactions as undefined.

Prior systems represent invalid states and react to violations. The present invention does not represent invalid interactions at all. Interactions that fall outside the admissible region are structurally nonexistent and therefore cannot be executed.

This constitutes structural enforcement rather than policybased or reactive control.

7.5 Standing Algebra Integration (ΣR)

7.5.1 Binding Status

This section defines, at operational granularity, the integration of Standing Algebra (ΣR) into the Envelope and Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG) framework described in Sections 3–6. All constructs described herein are expressed exclusively using the primitives defined in Section 3 and are instantiated within the architecture of Section 4 and the Envelope model of Section 5.

The formal mathematical theory of Standing Algebra (ΣR), including its axioms, symbolic formulation, and proofs, is not claimed in isolation. The present invention claims the technical application of ΣR constraints, evaluated by a deterministic enforcement mechanism, to:

compute standingbased admissibility,

constrain permissible evolution of State,

guarantee structural exit viability,

and induce Envelope collapse when invariants cannot be satisfied.

7.5.2 Role of Standing Algebra Within the System

Standing Algebra defines the structural legitimacy conditions governing how relative authorization, influence, and dependency may evolve within an Envelope. It operates strictly as a preexecution admissibility filter.

Standing Algebra does not:

select actions,

optimize outcomes,

assign policies,

or perform posthoc evaluation.

Within the system:

the Envelope defines interaction scope,

ICG defines interaction geometry,

ΣR defines standing evolution and exit viability.

Admissibility requires simultaneous satisfaction of all three. No layer subsumes another.

7.5.3 Standing Algebra Reference, Attribution, and Availability

Standing Algebra (ΣR) is a formal, manysorted system describing standing, autonomy, recognition, and nondomination in multiparticipant systems, originally authored by the inventor, Jonathan Rademacher.

The formal system has been publicly documented and made available by the inventor, including through publicly accessible repositories. One such publication is available via Zenodo at:

https://zenodo.org/records/19656146

The referenced materials describe the abstract mathematical formulation of ΣR, including its axioms, symbolic structure, and formal properties, including:

successorbased, nondilutive standing (Tier 1),

capacityindexed autonomy,

autonomylimiting reflex,

nonreciprocity prevention,

bounded drift,

standing symmetry conditions,

and directed repair constraints (Tier 2).

The present invention does not claim the abstract mathematical theory of Standing Algebra. Instead, it claims the technical application of these constraints, as implemented by a deterministic enforcement mechanism within a computing system.

The inventor is the author of both the underlying formal framework and the present technical application. The disclosure provided herein is complete and selfcontained and does not require access to external materials for implementation.

7.5.4 Standing and Capacity State Encoding

For each Participant i, the System maintains a structured representation of standing:

σᵢ = ⟨cᵢ, sᵢ, rᵢ⟩

where:

cᵢ denotes standing class (null, prime, or composite),

sᵢ denotes successor index as a discrete ordinal,

rᵢ denotes bounded ratchet depth.

Standing is nonscalar, noncontinuous, and evolves only through admissible successor transitions.

For each Participant i, the System maintains capacity:

κᵢ = ⟨aᵢ, bᵢ, lᵢ⟩

where:

aᵢ is available capacity,

bᵢ is committed capacity,

lᵢ is nonrecoverable capacity loss.

Capacity constrains admissible transitions and bounds permissible evolution of standing.

7.5.5 Standing-Based Admissibility Evaluation Pipeline

Standing Algebra is evaluated for each proposed Operation F: S → S′, per Participant, prior to execution.

Evaluation is conjunctive. Failure at any stage renders F undefined.

For each Participant i, the enforcement mechanism performs:

projection of F onto i,

computation of Δσᵢ,

validation of successor legality,

validation of nondilution under composition,

validation of capacity sufficiency,

validation of reflex bounds,

validation of reciprocity relations,

validation of bounded accumulation,

validation of exit preservation,

intersection with ICG admissibility.

No Operation is admissible unless all conditions are satisfied simultaneously.

7.5.6 Tier 1 Constraints: NonDilutive Standing

Standing evolves through strictly bounded successor transitions:

Δσᵢ ∈ {0, +1}

Negative, fractional, or multisuccessor transitions are unrepresentable.

Across any admissible sequence:

standing class membership is invariant,

successor indices increase monotonically,

ratchet depth remains bounded.

Standing cannot be redistributed, averaged, merged, or erased. Any Operation implying such transformation is undefined.

7.5.7 Tier 2 Constraints: Capacity, Symmetry, and NonDomination

Capacityindexed autonomy constrains transitions:
