Δsᵢ ≤ f(κᵢ)

Operations exceeding available capacity are undefined.

The autonomylimiting reflex ensures no Operation reduces a Participant below its admissible standing bound. Selfinduced domination is structurally impossible.

Reciprocity constraints require that any increase in standing for one Participant admits structurally valid reciprocal relations for others. Absence of such reciprocity renders the Operation undefined.

Accumulation constraints enforce bounded drift. No admissible sequence produces unbounded asymmetry or cumulative erosion of autonomy.

7.5.8 Exit Viability

For each Participant i, there must exist an admissible exit mapping:

Eᵢ: S → Sₑ

such that:

Eᵢ consists only of admissible Operations,

σᵢ(Sₑ) ≥ σᵢ(S),

κᵢ is not exceeded,

i is not included in the resulting Participant Set.

Every admissible Operation must preserve at least one such mapping.

Any Operation that eliminates all exit mappings for any Participant is undefined.

If no exit mapping exists, the admissible region is empty and collapse is triggered.

7.5.9 Standing-Induced Collapse Semantics

Standing constraints induce collapse under three conditions:

no admissible standing transitions exist,

no exit mappings remain,

or standing constraints conflict with ICG geometry.

In all cases, the Envelope’s admissible region becomes empty. Collapse enforces invariants through termination of admissible space.

7.5.10 Integration with ICG and Envelope

ICG constrains geometric admissibility.
ΣR constrains relational admissibility.
The Envelope represents their intersection.

An Operation is admissible if and only if:

it lies within the admissible geometric region, and

it satisfies all standing and capacity constraints.

Neither framework alone is sufficient.

7.5.11 Structural Enforcement Properties

The integrated enforcement guarantees:

no admissible sequence produces irreversible asymmetric influence,

no Participant loses exit viability,

no admissible path leads to structural domination,

admissibility is invariant across time,

no history or learning influences admissibility,

all admissible transitions preserve closure.

All enforcement is preexecution and structural.

7.5.12 Domain Independence

The standing constraint system applies uniformly across:

data systems,

device control,

service execution,

AI interaction flows,

authentication systems.

It constrains admissibility of interactions, not internal optimization or model behavior.

7.5.13 Novel Construction

The novelty resides in the mechanical integration of:

geometric admissibility (ICG),

standingbased admissibility (ΣR),

and exitpreserving closure semantics,

within a single deterministic enforcement mechanism.

Domination, coercion, and irreversible dependency are not represented system states.

Prior systems permit such states and respond after occurrence. The present invention structurally prevents their representation.

7.7 Sensor-Level Biometric Authentication (Envelope-Protected, Geometry-Enforced)

7.7.1 Binding Status

Biometric authentication is implemented as an Envelope-mediated interaction system in which sensing, validation, and admissibility determination occur exclusively within Envelope-bounded contexts.

All biometric sensing interactions are subject to:

Envelope-based admissibility,

geometric constraint evaluation under the interoperability constraint framework,

and standing-based admissibility and exit viability constraints.

No biometric signal, representation, or derived artifact exists within the System unless it arises from an admissible sensing interaction.

All constructs used herein are derived exclusively from previously defined primitives. No new semantic primitives are introduced.

7.7.2 Architectural Role of Sensor-Level Authentication

Biometric authentication is treated as an interaction geometry rather than a feature extraction or comparison process.

Let:

denote physical sensor input

denote an Interaction representation

denote a derived Operation candidate

denote the admissibility function

Then biometric processing is constrained as:

with the condition:

No recognition, identification, or matching occurs prior to admissibility determination.

Authentication is therefore enforced by structural admissibility rather than comparison to stored templates.

mediation of all physical-to-digital interaction,

admissibility evaluation prior to any signal propagation,

elimination of raw sensor data exposure.

The sensor is treated as a Participant subject to admissibility constraints.

7.22 User Interface Layer and Admissible Interaction Surfaces (Non-Interrogative Interfaces, Non-Dominant Interaction, Relevance-Bound Admissibility, Temporal Null Semantics, and Standing-Preserving Human–System Coupling)

7.22.1 Function of the User Interface Layer

7.22.1.1 Structural Role

The User Interface Layer (UIL) defines the only admissible boundary through which external agents may interact with the system. This boundary connects:

human agents,

biometric sensing components,

device execution environments,

and the Construction Environment.

The UIL does not participate in authentication, authorization, or admissibility determination. It does not evaluate input, interpret identity, or influence system state.

Instead, the UIL exists solely to permit the initiation of an interaction attempt. This attempt is forwarded into the system for evaluation under the constraint framework defined by the Construction Environment, Interoperability Constraint Geometry, and temporal coherence rules.

Formally, the UIL permits only:

All outcomes are determined entirely upstream. The UIL is not a decision point; it is a boundary through which interaction is initiated.

7.22.1.2 Necessity Under System Invariants

The constraints applied to the UIL are necessary because, without them, prohibited system behaviors could be reintroduced through interface design. In particular:

feedback mechanisms can encode identity recognition,

prompts can introduce coercion,

decision indicators can introduce dominance,

and graded responses can expose hidden evaluation criteria.

These risks directly violate the system’s invariants, including:

non-dominance (no component may unilaterally determine outcomes),

non-coercion (no requirement for user proof or response),

binary relevance (no ranking or scoring),

temporal invariance (no adaptive behavior over time).

The UIL is therefore constrained so that no such behaviors can be represented within the system.

7.22.2 Definition of Admissible Interaction

7.22.2.1 Attempt Semantics

The only admissible action at the UIL is an attempt:

An attempt is defined as a non-interrogative submission of interaction data. It is not:

a request for access,

a claim of identity,

a proof of knowledge or possession,

or an input evaluated at the interface layer.

An attempt produces exactly one of two outcomes:

No intermediate states, partial results, or progressive outcomes exist.

7.22.2.2 Absence of Dialogical Interaction

The system does not admit dialog-based interaction sequences. There is no mechanism by which the system:

asks follow-up questions,

requests additional input,

adapts based on prior responses,

or engages in multi-step authentication flows.

Formally, no function exists of the form:

based on system output.

This eliminates any feedback loop between system output and subsequent input, which would otherwise enable iterative probing or coercion.

7.22.3 UIL as a Projection Layer

7.22.3.1 Projection Semantics

The UIL is defined as a projection from system state to observable output:

where:

represents internal system state,

represents externally observable behavior.

The UIL cannot modify system state. There exists no function:

This ensures that the interface cannot influence admissibility, existence, or authorization outcomes.

7.22.3.2 Absence of Causal Standing

The UIL has no causal role in determining whether an Envelope exists. There is no path from the UIL to the admissibility function:

where evaluates constraint satisfaction.

All evaluation occurs independently of the interface. The UIL cannot:

grant or deny access,

influence constraint evaluation,

modify temporal coherence,

or affect ledger state.

7.22.3.3 Read-Only Observability

The UIL may observe only whether downstream system functionality becomes accessible or remains unchanged.

It may not observe:

reasons for failure,

details of admissibility evaluation,

lineage or ledger state,

temporal coherence conditions.

This ensures that no output of the UIL can be used to infer internal system state.

7.22.4 Prohibited Interface Semantics

7.22.4.1 Absence of Authentication Language

The system does not produce outputs that explicitly describe authentication outcomes, such as:

“authentication failed,”

“access denied,”

“incorrect input,”

or “try again.”

Such outputs would reveal internal evaluation results and create an oracle that could be probed by an attacker.

Instead, all failure conditions produce the same observable result: absence of effect.

7.22.4.2 Absence of Explicit Success Signaling

The system does not explicitly signal successful authentication.

Instead, success is observable only indirectly, through the availability of downstream functionality that requires an existing Envelope.

This ensures that success is not exposed as a distinct signal that can be measured or exploited.

7.22.4.3 Absence of Progressive Feedback

The UIL does not provide graded or progressive feedback, including:

quality indicators,

progress bars,

biometric previews,

or any form of scoring.

Such feedback would create a gradient that could be used to iteratively refine inputs and reverse-engineer admissibility conditions.

7.22.4.4 Non-Dominance in User Interaction

The UIL has no authority over system outcomes. It cannot:

grant access,

deny access,

elevate privileges,

or modify standing.

All such actions would require the UIL to override constraint-based existence, which is structurally prohibited.

Similarly, the system does not admit override mechanisms such as:

administrative access paths,

emergency credentials,

or bypass controls.

Such mechanisms would introduce unilateral authority and violate the non-dominance invariant.

7.22.4.5 Non-Coercion in User Interaction

The UIL does not require users to provide proofs of identity, knowledge, or possession.

The system does not prompt for:

passwords,

PINs,

tokens,

or any form of secret input.

All evaluation is based on coherence of the interaction itself, not on user-provided assertions.

Users are not required to interact beyond initiating an attempt, and no penalty or escalation occurs if they do not.

7.22.4.6 Relevance Without Ranking

The system enforces binary relevance:

There is no ranking, scoring, or threshold-based evaluation exposed at the interface level.

The UIL does not guide users toward successful input or provide feedback that would enable optimization.

7.22.4.7 Temporal Semantics Without Dynamics

Each interaction attempt is treated independently at the interface layer.

The UIL does not:

adapt based on prior attempts,

remember failures,

or change behavior over time.

Additionally, the system does not expose timing differences that correlate with internal state.

Time is used only for ordering within the ledger and is not observable through the interface.

7.22.4.8 Device-Level Interaction Constraints

Biometric sensors operate under strict constraints:

measurements are captured,

immediately forwarded for evaluation,

and then discarded.

Sensors do not:

store data locally,

provide previews,

or replay prior inputs.

The UIL does not expose any feedback about biometric quality or structure, preventing leakage of geometric information.

7.22.4.9 Observability and Side-Channel Neutrality

All invalid interaction attempts produce identical observable outcomes, regardless of the cause of failure.

The system ensures that no observable variation exists in:

timing,

response behavior,

or resource usage.

This prevents side-channel attacks that could reveal internal system state.

7.22.4.10 Failure Semantics

Failure is defined as:

absence of Envelope existence,

absence of system state change,

absence of observable signal.

This is expressed as:

Retries are permitted but receive no guidance or feedback, ensuring that repeated attempts do not form an adaptive interaction.

7.22.4.11 Interaction Boundary with Construction Environment and Ledger

The UIL cannot access or query the ledger or Construction Environment state.

There is no mechanism by which the UIL can:

determine lineage existence,

detect revocation,

or infer temporal coherence.

The UIL can initiate an attempt but cannot observe the causal process that determines its outcome.

7.22.4.12 Sufficiency and Closure

The constraints defined in this section are sufficient to ensure that:

no identity recognition interface exists,

no interactive attack surface is exposed,

no coercive interaction is possible,

and no dominant behavior can arise through the interface.

Together with the system definition and boundary conditions, this establishes a closed system in which interaction cannot be used to extract, influence, or probe authentication behavior.

7.22.5 Execution Normalization, FixedPoint Enforcement, and ConstraintInduced Ordering

7.22.5.A. Execution Normalization and Admissibility Projection

In the described architecture, inputs, proposals, interaction attempts, or execution requests are not evaluated or executed in their raw form.
Instead, each such structure is first subjected to a normalization or projection process that maps the structure into an admissible canonical form determined by the constraint system.

Only the normalized structure is eligible to participate in execution, interaction, or state transition.
If no admissible canonical form exists for a given input or proposal, no execution context is instantiated and no system effect occurs.

This projection mechanism ensures that execution does not depend on representational form, construction history, or external labeling, and that no system behavior occurs outside the admissible space defined by the constraint geometry.

7.22.5.B. FixedPoint Execution and ClosureBased State Stability

In the described system, admissible execution states correspond to fixed points of the constraintpreserving transformation process.
A system state is permitted to persist or produce effects only if application of the admissibility and normalization processes yields the same state.

States that do not satisfy this fixedpoint condition are not stabilized and do not produce persistent system effects.
As a result, execution converges deterministically toward admissible fixed points and cannot remain in transient, partially admissible, or noncompliant states.

This fixedpoint enforcement provides closure under admissible transformation and prevents bypass of constraint enforcement through iterative execution, repeated interaction, or adversarial sequencing.

7.22.5.C. ConstraintInduced Ordering of System Evolution

The constraint system induces a partial ordering over admissible structures based on satisfaction of invariant conditions.
This ordering is not a ranking, optimization, or preference function, but a structural relation that determines whether one state represents a refinement, repair, or stabilization of another.

System evolution proceeds monotonically with respect to this ordering: transitions that reduce constraint satisfaction are not instantiated, while transitions that preserve or improve admissibility may occur.

This constraintinduced ordering enables directed repair, stabilization, and convergence without optimization, scoring, or selection, and ensures that system evolution remains bounded within the admissible space.

7.22.6 Non-Normative Appendix

Explanatory metaphors may be used to aid understanding but do not define system behavior or claims. The system defines a primitive evaluative operation, herein referred to as a constraint membership predicate, which determines whether a relational geometry derived from an interaction belongs to the constrained admissible state space. This predicate replaces conventional matching functions and operates without comparison to stored references.

7.23 Distortion, Attack, and Collapse Handling (Geometric Distortion Resolution, Attack as Inadmissible Construction, Automatic Closure via Non-Existence, and Absence of Detection or Mitigation Semantics)

7.23.1 Purpose and Scope

This section defines the structural handling of distortion, adversarial behavior, misuse, and attack within the system. The system does not implement attack detection, attacker classification, mitigation strategies, escalation paths, or reactive security behavior. Instead, all such conditions are resolved exclusively through the same admissibility and existence semantics governing all interaction outcomes.

All outcomes are determined solely by evaluation of the constraint membership predicate applied to relational geometry derived from interaction. The system does not distinguish between benign and adversarial causes, and no semantic attribution is performed. All distortions and attacks are therefore treated uniformly as inadmissible constructions that fail to instantiate existence.

Let an interaction produce a relational geometry . Admissibility is determined exclusively by the constraint membership predicate:

This predicate constitutes the sole evaluative mechanism within the system. No alternative function exists that determines admissibility through comparison, classification, scoring, or interpretation. All distortion, attack, and misuse correspond to cases where .

7.23.2 Structural Definition of Distortion

Structural distortion is defined as any deviation of input, context, mediation, timing, or relational structure such that the resulting relational geometry fails to satisfy invariant constraints or violates temporal coherence within the Construction Environment. Formally:

Distortion(G)⟺¬(G⊨C) ∨ Contradiction(G,L)

Distortion is therefore a property of structure and system state, not of intent.

All distortions are treated as geometric deformations. These include, without limitation, spatial deformation of relational structures, temporal misalignment or reordering, disruption of continuity or dependency relations, insertion of intermediary transformations, and recomposition of partial or stale measurements. Because evaluation is performed exclusively on relational invariants, any such deformation prevents the derivation of a coherent geometry admissible under the constraint system.

No semantic attribution is performed. The system does not distinguish between accidental distortion, environmental noise, benign misuse, or adversarial manipulation. No classification function exists that maps interaction inputs to categories such as “attack” or “error.” All inputs are evaluated solely through structural admissibility, and any input that fails to satisfy the constraint membership predicate does not produce existence.

7.23.3 Illustrative Distortion and Attack Scenarios

Illustrative interaction scenarios demonstrate the structural consequences of distortion without defining detection logic.

In piggybacking or parasitic interaction, additional signals or processes are introduced into an otherwise valid interaction. This introduces inconsistencies in timing, dependency, or continuity relations. As a result:

and no execution structure is instantiated.

In capture, recording, or reproduction scenarios, biometric interactions are recorded and replayed. Such reproduced inputs lack live temporal coherence, device binding, and Construction Environment consistency. Therefore:

and no admissible existence event occurs.

In interposition scenarios, including relay, modification, or delay of signals, relational geometry is distorted by transformation or latency. This produces violations of invariant constraints:

In replay scenarios, previously valid interaction sequences are reused. These violate temporal ordering and non-forking constraints defined by the ledger:

In all such cases, the system does not detect or classify the attack. It evaluates only admissibility.

7.23.4 Automatic Closure Semantics

The system contains no detection logic. It does not implement intrusion detection systems, anomaly detection, behavioral heuristics, or fraud classification. Any such mechanism would introduce an evaluative authority capable of interpreting input, which is structurally prohibited by the system’s invariants.

The system also contains no mitigation logic. It does not perform blocking, throttling, quarantine, countermeasures, or adaptive defensive behavior. No mitigation is required because no inadmissible construction produces system state.

All inadmissible constructions resolve through automatic collapse to non-existence. Formally:

where denotes the existence of an execution structure. Under this condition:

no execution structure exists,

no partial or intermediate state persists,

no system mode changes occur,

no observable signal is emitted.

Collapse is immediate and final for the given interaction.

Because existence is binary, stateless across attempts, and non-accumulative, no exploit state can arise. For all inadmissible interactions:

where denotes system state. There is no compromised session, degraded mode, or persistent foothold. An exploit cannot persist because no state is instantiated in which persistence could occur.

7.23.5 Alignment with Structural Invariants

The system preserves non-dominance. No input, including adversarial input, gains standing by attempting distortion. No component gains authority by reacting. The system does not respond; it enforces invariants:

The system preserves non-coercion. It does not challenge, interrogate, accuse, or respond to inputs. Distorted inputs are not rejected through evaluation; they simply do not instantiate existence.

Temporal behavior remains invariant. Distortion does not induce learning, adaptation, policy change, or evolution of system behavior. The constraint system and evaluation mechanism remain constant over time:

The system does not record failed attempts, attacker behavior, or patterns of misuse. The ledger records only successful existence events. There is no accumulation of attack history.

Relevance is strictly binary. Each interaction yields either admissible existence or non-existence:

No ranking, scoring, or partial relevance is computed. Because no feedback is emitted, no gradient or optimization loop can form:

Thus, no adversarial process can iteratively refine inputs toward admissibility.

7.23.6 Observability and Side-Channel Neutrality

All failure modes are observationally indistinguishable. For all inadmissible geometries:

No observable variation in timing, resource consumption, power usage, or interface behavior reveals the cause of failure. Side-channel leakage is structurally prohibited:

¬∃Δt, Δr, Δp→failure classification

7.23.7 Sufficiency and Explicit Exclusions

The mechanisms defined herein are sufficient to eliminate interactive attack surfaces, prevent persistence of exploit states, prevent formation of feedback loops, and ensure that adversarial behavior cannot influence system evolution. No additional defensive mechanisms are required because no admissible exploit state exists.

The system explicitly excludes intrusion detection, behavioral analysis, fraud prevention logic, and adaptive security policies. Any such inclusion would introduce representational evaluation or interpretive authority and is therefore incompatible with the constraint-based existence model defined by the system.

7.24 Denial of Service Behavior, Recovery, Re-Instantiation, and Threat Model Closure (Availability Without Authority, Non-Mitigation Recovery, Existence-Preserving Continuity, and Elimination of Interactive Exhaustion Surfaces)

7.24.1 Purpose and Scope

This section defines system behavior under conditions of resource exhaustion, denial of service, saturation, interference, or sustained interaction pressure. The system preserves non-dominance, non-coercion, non-forking existence, and the absence of interactive attack surfaces under all such conditions.

No detection, throttling, classification, mitigation, or reactive response mechanisms are introduced. Availability is handled exclusively through the same constraint-based existence semantics governing all interaction outcomes. This section completes the availability dimension of the system without introducing additional authority or evaluative pathways.

All interaction outcomes, including those under resource exhaustion, are determined solely by evaluation of the constraint membership predicate:

No mechanism exists that modifies, overrides, or supplements this predicate under conditions of resource pressure.

7.24.2 Definition of Denial of Service

Denial of Service is defined as any condition in which system resources are saturated, delayed, degraded, or unavailable, regardless of cause. DoS is not classified by intent and is not distinguished as malicious, adversarial, or accidental.

Within the system, DoS is expressed as availability distortion. Specifically, it manifests as a temporary inability to complete the instantiation of an execution structure, independent of whether the underlying relational geometry is admissible.

Formally, let denote the availability of required system resources. Then instantiation requires:

Under DoS conditions:

Thus, both admissible and inadmissible constructions may fail to instantiate under DoS, and all such failures resolve identically as non-existence.

7.24.3 Absence of Detection and Mitigation Semantics

The system does not include DoS detection mechanisms. It does not perform traffic analysis, rate-based evaluation, volumetric classification, or behavioral heuristics. No function exists of the form:

Such mechanisms would introduce evaluative authority and violate non-dominance.

The system also does not include mitigation semantics. It does not perform throttling, blacklisting, adaptive blocking, challenge escalation, prioritization, or penalty enforcement. No function exists that modifies system behavior based on observed interaction volume or frequency.

There is no attacker model within the system. There are only interaction attempts evaluated under admissibility and resource availability constraints.

7.24.4 Collapse Semantics Under Resource Exhaustion

Under conditions of resource exhaustion or unavailability, all instantiation attempts collapse to non-existence. Formally:

No distinction is made between failure due to inadmissibility and failure due to unavailability.

Under this condition:

no execution structure is instantiated,

no partial state persists,

no degraded or fallback mode is entered,

no system signal is emitted.

Collapse behavior under DoS is identical to all other failure modes, ensuring that no additional information is revealed.

7.24.5 Observational Indistinguishability

All failure outcomes are observationally indistinguishable. For all inputs such that instantiation does not occur:

This includes failures caused by:

resource exhaustion,

distortion,

adversarial interaction,

revoked lineage,

invalid environment,

or any other cause.

No observable property of system output, timing, or behavior distinguishes these conditions.

Side-channel leakage is structurally prohibited:

7.24.6 Recovery Without Mitigation

Recovery is defined solely as restoration of resource availability. No recovery logic is executed. The system does not perform restoration, replay, compensation, or correction.

When transitions from unavailable to available:

the system resumes normal operation without modification of state, behavior, or evaluation logic.

No memory of prior unavailability is retained. No state is restored because no state was instantiated during failure.

7.24.7 Re-Instantiation Semantics

Following restoration of availability, interaction attempts proceed under the same conditions as initial attempts. Re-instantiation is stateless:

No penalty, delay, reset, or re-enrollment is required. The constraint membership predicate is applied identically to all attempts.

The system does not implement queuing, prioritization, or ordering of attempts. No precedence is given to earlier or later interactions. All attempts are evaluated independently under relevance neutrality.

7.24.8 Alignment with Structural Invariants

Availability does not confer authority. No component gains standing during resource exhaustion or recovery. No administrative or privileged pathway exists to override resource constraints or grant selective access.

The system preserves non-coercion. It does not prompt users to retry, wait, prove legitimacy, or engage in additional interaction. Availability is not negotiated and does not introduce interaction semantics.

Temporal invariance is preserved. Resource exhaustion does not induce learning, adaptation, hardening, or policy evolution:

Repeated attempts under DoS conditions do not affect future admissibility. No cumulative penalties, markings, or behavioral memory are introduced.

7.24.9 Threat Model Closure

Attack classes that depend on interaction, feedback, or representation are structurally eliminated. These include credential stuffing, brute force authentication, password spraying, template extraction, oracle probing, adaptive replay, challenge-response exploitation, privilege escalation via interface, and account lockout abuse.

Such attacks require observable feedback or state accumulation, neither of which exists in the system.

Attacks based on volumetric pressure, including flooding and resource exhaustion, reduce solely to availability effects. They do not enable information extraction, access gain, state modification, or persistence.

7.24.10 Residual Conditions

Residual risks are limited to physical or external conditions, including device compromise, hardware faults, implementation defects, and supply chain vulnerabilities. These conditions exist outside the logical and computational model of the system.

No virtual or logical attack surface remains within the defined system boundaries.

7.24.11 Sufficiency and Closure

The behaviors defined in this section are sufficient to ensure that denial of service conditions cannot be transformed into an interactive attack vector. Resource exhaustion results only in temporary non-existence and cannot produce exploitable state, feedback, or authority.

No additional availability defenses are required. Any mechanism introducing detection, mitigation, or adaptive response would violate the constraint-based existence model and reintroduce prohibited evaluative pathways.

8.  Fatal Implementation Shortcuts, Failure Modes, and Invalid Embodiments (Implementation Prohibitions, Structural Failure Classification, Invalid Shortcut Definition, and Preservation of Non-Dominance and Non-Coercion)

8.1 Purpose and Examiner-Facing Rationale

This section defines implementation shortcuts, simplifications, and substitutions that may appear reasonable to a skilled practitioner but that violate the structural invariants of the system. The purpose of this section is to ensure that the invention is implemented in a manner consistent with its defining principles and to explicitly exclude foreseeable incorrect embodiments.

The system is defined by invariant properties including non-representational identity, non-comparative evaluation, non-dominance, non-coercion, binary admissibility, and non-forking temporal coherence. Any implementation that violates these invariants, even if superficially functional, does not constitute a valid embodiment of the invention.

This section ensures that a skilled person is informed not only how to implement the system, but also how not to implement it, thereby satisfying sufficiency and clarity requirements. It further ensures that the scope of protection cannot be undermined by obvious but incorrect substitutions.

8.2 Definition of Structural Failure

A fatal implementation shortcut is defined as any design decision that preserves superficial system behavior while violating one or more structural invariants. Such invariants include, without limitation:

non-dominance,

non-coercion,

non-forking existence,

observational indistinguishability,

binary admissibility,

and exclusive reliance on the constraint membership predicate.

A structural failure is distinct from an implementation error. Implementation errors occur within a valid architecture and do not negate the underlying model. Structural failures alter the architecture such that the defining behavior of the system is no longer preserved.

Formally, let the system be defined by predicate . A valid embodiment requires:

Any implementation introducing an alternative or auxiliary evaluative function:

constitutes a structural failure.

8.3 Identity Re-Introduction Failures

Any persistence of canonical geometry, feature vectors, or relational representations derived from interaction constitutes a structural violation. If a relational geometry or any function of is stored in a manner enabling reuse or comparison, the system transitions from constraint-based existence evaluation to representational identity.

Formally, if:

then comparison becomes possible, and the system collapses into a matching-based architecture.

Similarly, hashing or encoding relational geometry as a surrogate identifier constitutes a violation. Even if irreversibility is preserved, equivalence classes are maintained:

This enables replay, identity portability, and comparison. Any such mechanism invalidates the embodiment.

8.4 Comparison and Threshold Failures

Any introduction of similarity scoring, distance metrics, tolerance thresholds, or approximate matching violates binary admissibility. Such mechanisms define a function:

with acceptance based on thresholding. This introduces gradients:

which enables optimization and probing.

Acceptance of “close enough” inputs transforms admissibility from a binary predicate into a ranked evaluation, violating relevance neutrality:

Any such implementation constitutes a structural failure.

8.5 Ledger Misuse Failures

The ledger must not be used as an identity store. Storing identifiers, references, or user-linked data introduces representational semantics:

This enables enumeration, probing, and indirect comparison.

Similarly, any mechanism allowing query access to ledger state violates one-way causality. If an entity can evaluate:

then the ledger becomes an oracle, enabling inference of admissibility conditions.

8.6 User Interface Authority and Feedback Failures

The User Interface Layer must not perform any evaluative or decision-making function. Any implementation where the UI determines acceptance or rejection introduces authority:

This violates non-dominance.

Similarly, any error messaging, diagnostic feedback, or differentiation of failure introduces observable structure:

which enables probing and inference. Such behavior constitutes an oracle and invalidates the embodiment.

8.7 Adaptive and Stateful Behavior Failures

The system must not retain memory of failed attempts or adapt behavior based on prior inputs. Any function:

violates temporal invariance.

Rate limiting, if implemented in a manner that signals failure, suspicion, or classification, introduces timing or behavioral side channels:

Such mechanisms introduce coercion and authority and are prohibited.

8.8 Genesis and Lineage Violations

The system enforces a non-forking existence model. Any mechanism permitting creation of new construction lineages outside controlled genesis violates this invariant:

This introduces parallel existence states and invalidates temporal coherence.

Similarly, implicit or automatic enrollment mechanisms that create lineage upon first success introduce identity semantics and uncontrolled genesis, violating the construction model.

8.9 Availability and Degraded Mode Violations

Any implementation that introduces degraded, fallback, or partial-trust modes violates binary existence:

Similarly, throttling or availability mechanisms that differentiate between inputs introduce mitigation semantics and observable classification, violating the absence of detection and mitigation logic.

8.10 Acceptable and Unacceptable Failure Domains

Certain failure domains are external to the system and do not invalidate the architecture. These include hardware faults, implementation defects, physical compromise, and external environmental conditions. Such failures do not alter the underlying constraint-based model.

In contrast, any failure that introduces identity representation, comparison, authority, feedback, or adaptive behavior constitutes a structural violation and results in an invalid embodiment.

8.11 Negative Definition of Excluded Embodiments

The following classes of systems are explicitly excluded:

biometric matching systems,

template-based authentication systems,

similarity or distance-based recognition systems,

challenge–response authentication schemes,

adaptive or learning-based security systems,

policy-driven access control systems.

These systems rely on mechanisms explicitly prohibited by the constraint-based existence model.

8.12 Sufficiency and Closure

The definitions provided herein are sufficient to ensure that a skilled person can distinguish valid embodiments from invalid implementations. Foreseeable shortcuts are explicitly identified and excluded, preventing erosion of the invention through partial or incorrect implementation.

The system is defined not only by what it performs, but by what it structurally cannot perform. Any implementation that introduces representational identity, comparison, authority, feedback, or adaptive behavior falls outside the scope of the invention, regardless of apparent functionality.

All embodiments described herein are subject to the prohibitions and invalid embodiment definitions established in Section 17.

9.4 Embodiment Class: Data Security Systems

Persistent state consists of protected data stored within structurally isolated data envelopes. Data may be encrypted or otherwise secured, but no access control lists, user identifiers, or permission tables are used.

Data access is attempted through instantiation of an execution structure. The relational geometry incorporates biometric interaction, device state, and Construction Environment coherence. No identity query is performed.

If an envelope exists, data becomes reachable. If no envelope exists, no data interaction occurs. Failure produces no observable output.

9.5 Embodiment Class: Regulated Data Systems

Persistent state consists of regulated records, including financial, healthcare, or governmental data. Data state is structurally separated from existence state.

Audit records may exist but must not contain identity representations or biometric artifacts.

Envelope existence governs access. Revocation removes admissibility without deleting data. No compliance decision or reasoning is surfaced.

9.6 Embodiment Class: Device Security and Control

Persistent state consists of device configuration, firmware state, or service activation state. No passwords, secrets, or user accounts are stored.

Interaction geometry combines biometric presence and device context. The device acts as a portal into the Construction Environment and does not act as an authority.

Device services activate only through Envelope existence. Failed attempts leave device state unchanged. No lockout, warning, or retry semantics are introduced.

9.7 Embodiment Class: Service and Output Governance

Persistent state consists of generated outputs, service responses, or computational results. Outputs are not precomputed or associated with identities.

Requests are expressed as attempts to instantiate output envelopes. Geometry may incorporate task context and environmental parameters but excludes identity.

Outputs materialize only if an envelope exists. Otherwise, no output is generated. No refusal, explanation, or justification is provided.

9.8 Embodiment Class: Policy Enforcement Substrate

Policy is encoded as structural constraints within , not as rule evaluation.

No policy engine evaluates identity, roles, or permissions. Policy outcomes are determined solely through admissibility.

If , the policy outcome manifests as existence. If , no outcome occurs. No policy decision is observable, and no override mechanism exists.

9.10 Embodiment Class: Constraint-Governed Structural Compression via Canonical Equivalence, Invariant Admissibility, and Referential Structural Reuse

9.10.1 System Construction and Representation Basis

In certain embodiments, a data representation system is defined in which compression arises from the manner in which representations are permitted to exist within a constraint-governed structural space, rather than from transformation of representations into shorter encodings. An input representation is therefore not treated as a fixed sequence of symbols, but is interpreted as a constructible relational structure under Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG) and Standing Algebra .

Under this interpretation, the representation admits decomposition into structural primitives, admissible relations, and governing constraints, such that the representation is not a singular encoding but a structural configuration capable of being expressed in multiple equivalent forms. The system therefore does not preserve the original encoding of , but instead evaluates the structure presented by against the admissibility conditions defined by the constraint system.

Where the representation contains repeated primitives, redundant relational constructions, or constraints that are implied by other constraints, such elements do not persist as independent components. Rather, the representation resolves into a structural form in which only those elements required for admissibility remain. In this sense, compression does not operate by removing data, but by constraining the form in which data is allowed to exist.

9.10.2 Canonical Equivalence and Structural Collapse

Within the constraint-governed representation space, equivalence is defined at the level of structure. Two representations and are equivalent if they produce the same canonical structure under admissible transformation:

This definition induces equivalence classes over the representation space, where each class corresponds to a single admissible structural configuration. For any representation , the system determines:

which is not a normalized encoding, but the only admissible structural form corresponding to that equivalence class.

Representations that differ in encoding but not in structure do not exist as distinct states within the system. They collapse into the same canonical structure , and no multiplicity of representation is retained. Compression therefore begins with the elimination of representational redundancy at the level of equivalence.

9.10.3 Invariant Minimality of Admissible Structures

The system does not perform selection among multiple candidate representations, nor does it evaluate alternative encodings for efficiency. Instead, minimality is enforced as an invariant of admissibility. A representation is admissible if and only if it contains no structural components beyond those required to satisfy the governing constraints.

If a representation contains additional primitives, relations, or constraints that do not contribute to admissibility, such that the structure is not irreducible, the constraint system is violated:

and the representation does not instantiate within the system.

Accordingly, non-minimal structures are not reduced; they are excluded. The canonical structure is therefore not chosen as the smallest among valid alternatives, but exists as the only representation that satisfies admissibility without redundancy.

9.10.4 Internal Structural Reduction and Non-Existence of Redundancy

The same admissibility condition applies within the internal composition of . Structural primitives are not duplicated where equivalence exists; if two primitives are indistinguishable under canonicalization, they are not separately instantiated. Relations are constrained such that any relation derivable from other relations does not independently exist. Constraints that are implied by other constraints are likewise not represented redundantly.

The resulting structure is therefore not simplified through removal, but defined such that redundant components cannot exist. At every level—primitive, relational, and constraint—the representation consists only of what is necessary for admissibility.

9.10.5 Partial Structural Reuse and Referential Instantiation

In addition to the collapse of entire representations, the system exhibits compression through reuse of partial structural configurations. Where a representation contains a substructure that is itself admissible and canonical, that substructure resolves to a single canonical instance:

Subsequent occurrences of structures equivalent to do not produce new instances, but are expressed through referential inclusion of .

This referential behavior arises from the same admissibility condition that prevents duplication. A structure that is equivalent to an existing canonical substructure cannot instantiate as a distinct entity without violating invariant minimality. Instead, the representation incorporates the existing structure through a relation expressing inclusion without re-instantiation.

As a result, repeated structural patterns—such as formatting configurations, layout schemas, or commonly occurring relational sequences—are stored once and reused across representations. Each new representation therefore consists only of those elements that are not already present within the admissible structural space.

9.10.6 Structural Scaling Behavior and Illustrative Example

The effect of this structural representation may be understood through an illustrative example involving an extended document, such as a book-length text comprising on the order of tens of thousands of words.

In a conventional system, such a document is stored as a continuous sequence of characters, and storage size scales linearly with the number of words, such that each occurrence of a word contributes independently to total size.

In the present system, the same document is represented as a composition of primitives and relations. Each unique word corresponds to a structural primitive defined once, regardless of how many times it appears. The document content is then expressed as a sequence relation over these primitives, which specifies ordering without duplicating the content of each word.

Where words repeat, their storage cost does not scale with frequency, but remains constant, as each occurrence is represented by a reference to the same primitive. Furthermore, where sequences of words or higher-order structures recur, such sequences may themselves resolve into canonical substructures and be referenced as single units rather than re-expressed in full.

As the system accumulates representations, the set of canonical primitives and substructures stabilizes, and new representations increasingly consist of references to existing structures. Accordingly, storage growth becomes dependent on the introduction of novel structure rather than on the repetition of existing structure.

The compression advantage therefore arises not from reducing the size of individual elements, but from eliminating repeated instantiation of identical or equivalent structures across the representation space.

9.10.7 Reconstruction from Canonical Structure

The canonical structure , together with any referenced substructures, constitutes a complete and sufficient specification of the admissible structure from which representations may be generated. Reconstruction is therefore defined not as recovery of lost data, but as evaluation of a fully specified structural definition.

A reconstruction function is defined:

where specifies the desired output form.

Reconstruction proceeds by resolving each primitive to its canonical definition, resolving references to canonical substructures, applying the relations that define ordering and composition, and enforcing the constraint system governing admissibility and formatting.

For example, where a document contains prose, the textual content is not stored as a continuous string but as a sequence of primitives representing linguistic units. The ordering relation defines the sequence in which those primitives are arranged. During reconstruction, the primitives are resolved and combined according to this relation, producing the corresponding textual sequence deterministically.

Formatting and layout are likewise represented as canonical substructures, which are referenced and applied during reconstruction rather than redefined for each instance.

Because all primitives, relations, and constraints required for admissibility are preserved within , reconstruction does not require inference, approximation, or recovery of missing information. The reconstructed representation satisfies:

and therefore belongs to the same equivalence class as the original input.

The system guarantees that no information required for admissible structure is lost. The absence of the original serialized representation reflects a change in representation, not a loss of content.

9.10.8 Distinction from Conventional Compression Mechanisms

The described system differs fundamentally from conventional compression methods in that compression is not achieved through encoding or transformation of representations. Instead, it arises from the definition of a representation space in which redundant structures—whether complete or partial—are not admissible as independent instances.

Compression is therefore a property of structural existence under constraints, rather than a process applied to representations.

9.10.9 System-Level Efficiency Effects

By enforcing canonical admissibility and referential reuse, the system reduces storage, indexing, and transmission requirements. Storage is limited to canonical structures and substructures, eliminating duplication at both global and local levels. Indexing operates over canonical identifiers, reducing lookup complexity. Transmission operates on canonical structures, with reconstruction performed at the receiving system.

9.10.10 Boundary Conditions and Applicability

Where input data admits structural equivalence or partial structural reuse, compression is realized through canonical collapse and referential inclusion. Where no such equivalence exists, the system produces a unique canonical structure without further reduction. In all cases, correctness is preserved, as only admissible structures are represented.

9.15 Embodiment Class: Closure-Constrained Computational Evaluation and Deterministic State-Space Restriction

In certain embodiments, the closure-based framework defined by Interoperability Constraint Geometry (ICG), Standing Algebra (ΣR), and Envelope construction is applied to restrict computational evaluation to a deterministically defined admissible state space. In this embodiment, the Envelope is not used to derive canonical representations or to perform constraint canonicalization, but instead functions as a structural operator that bounds the domain of computational evaluation.

Let a computational process be defined over a candidate state space S, where elements of S represent possible states, actions, or solution paths to be evaluated by a computational system. In conventional systems, evaluation proceeds over S through iterative, combinatorial, or heuristic exploration, including search, optimization, planning, or agent-based evaluation. Such processes typically include evaluation of elements that are later determined to be invalid or non-viable.

In the present embodiment, the constraint system defined by ICG and ΣR induces an admissible subset S* ⊆ S, where S* corresponds exactly to the closure-defined Envelope E. The Envelope therefore defines a complete admissible state space such that:

E = closure(C) ⊆ S

where C is the constraint set derived from relational geometry under ICG and ΣR.

A computational evaluation operator Φ, which would conventionally operate over S, is instead restricted to operate over E such that:

Φ: E → E'

and is undefined for all elements outside E. As a result, any candidate state s ∉ E is not instantiated, evaluated, or propagated within the computational process.

This restriction is enforced structurally by constructing evaluation pipelines, execution contexts, or computational graphs such that admissibility under E is a precondition for state formation. In particular:

• candidate states are generated only through constraint-consistent transformations;
• transitions are permitted only if closure is preserved under ICG and ΣR;
• evaluation functions are defined only over admissible domains;
• and no post hoc rejection, filtering, or corrective rollback is required for inadmissible states.

Unlike constraint solving or canonicalization, this embodiment does not compute admissibility during evaluation. Instead, admissibility is pre-established through Envelope construction, and computational processes are confined to the resulting admissible domain.

The technical effect of this construction is a reduction in the effective cardinality of the evaluated state space. For computational processes characterized by combinatorial or high-dimensional candidate spaces, this results in:

• reduction in the number of candidate states requiring evaluation;
• reduction in processor cycles associated with evaluation operations;
• reduction in memory access and intermediate state storage;
• reduction in control-plane overhead associated with validation and rollback;
• and corresponding reduction in energy consumption.

This embodiment is particularly applicable to computational systems in which invalid or non-viable states constitute a significant proportion of the candidate space, including planning systems, scheduling and resource allocation systems, distributed coordination systems, agent-based computation, and constraint-bound decision processes.

The restriction of evaluation to closure-admissible domains is independent of the specific algorithms, models, or optimization techniques employed within Φ. The underlying computational semantics of the system are preserved, while the evaluation domain is structurally reduced.

This embodiment operates strictly downstream of constraint construction, canonicalization, and structural verification, and does not modify the definition of admissibility or the formation of constraint sets. Instead, it enforces admissibility as a boundary condition on computational evaluation, thereby transforming the operational characteristics of the system from unconstrained exploration to closure-bounded computation.
